=== BUGGY CODE ===
from collections import defaultdict
class Solution:
    def maxPoints(self, points: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        N = len(points)
        D = defaultdict(set)
        if N <= 2:
            return N 
        for i in range(N):
            x1, y1 = points[i]
            for j in range(i+1, N):
                x2, y2 = points[j]
                if y1 = y2:
                    k1, k2 = 'inf', y1
                elif x1 = x2:
                    k1, k2 = x1, 'inf'
                else:
                    k1, k2 = (x1-x2)/(y2-y1), (x2*y1-x1*y2)/(y2-y1)
                D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))
                D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[j]))
        return max([len(i) for i in D.values()])

- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;

```
from collections import defaultdict
class Solution:
    def maxPoints(self, points: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        N = len(points)
        D = defaultdict(set)
        if N <= 2:
            return N 
        for i in range(N):
            x1, y1 = points[i]
            for j in range(i+1, N):
                x2, y2 = points[j]
                if y1 = y2:
                    k1, k2 = 'inf', y1
                elif x1 = x2:
                    k1, k2 = x1, 'inf'
                else:
                    k1, k2 = (x1-x2)/(y2-y1), (x2*y1-x1*y2)/(y2-y1)
                D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))
                D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[j]))
        return max([len(i) for i in D.values()])
```

```
from collections import defaultdict
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxPoints(self, points: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        N = len(points)
        if N <= 2:
            return N

        D = defaultdict(set)

        for i in range(N):
            x1, y1 = points[i]
            for j in range(i+1, N):
                x2, y2 = points[j]
                if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2:
                    continue
                if y1 == y2:
                    k1, k2 = 'inf', y1
                elif x1 == x2:
                    k1, k2 = x1, 'inf'
                else:
                    k1, k2 = (x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1), (x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)
                D[(k1, k2)].add(i)
                D[(k1, k2)].add(j)

        if not D:
            return N
        return max(len(v) for v in D.values())
```

Original:
```
from collections import defaultdict
class Solution:
    def maxPoints(self, points: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        N = len(points)
        D = defaultdict(set)
        if N <= 2:
            return N 
        for i in range(N):
            x1, y1 = points[i]
            for j in range(i+1, N):
                x2, y2 = points[j]
                if y1 = y2:
                    k1, k2 = 'inf', y1
                elif x1 = x2:
                    k1, k2 = x1, 'inf'
                else:
                    k1, k2 = (x1-x2)/(y2-y1), (x2*y1-x1*y2)/(y2-y1)
                D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))
                D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[j]))
        return max([len(i) for i in D.values()])
```

Now, let's think about if the final code should be exactly:
```
from collections import defaultdict
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxPoints(self, points: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        N = len(points)
        if N <= 2:
            return N

        D = defaultdict(set)

        for i in range(N):
            x1, y1 = points[i]
            for j in range(i + 1, N):
                x2, y2 = points[j]
                if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2:
                    continue

                if y1 == y2:
                    k1, k2 = 'inf', y1
                elif x1 == x2:
                    k1, k2 = x1, 'inf'
                else:
                    k1, k2 = (x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1), (x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)

                D[(k1, k2)].add(i)
                D[(k1, k2)].add(j)

        if not D:
            return N

        return max(len(v) for v in D.values())
```
This is good.

But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal lines if y1 is an int and for vertical lines if x1 is an int, but for nonvertical/nonhorizontal k1/k2 are floats. Python tuple equality between int and float works if values equal, but string vs float no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the original code's `D = defaultdict(set)` and `return max([len(i) for i in D.values()])` would fail if D empty due to all identical. We fixed.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if not D: return N` but if N=2 and points distinct, D nonempty but return N before. If N=2 and points identical, return N. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is `-0.0`; hash equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `points[i][0] == points[j][0] and points[i][1] == points[j][1]` to skip duplicates. We use x1,y1. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further optimized by not storing all lines but updating max. Not necessary.

Now, let's consider if there is a potential bug with the line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical if `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a float that is not exactly the same for different pairs due to floating point rounding. We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal lines if y1 is an int and for vertical lines if x1 is an int, but for nonvertical/nonhorizontal k1/k2 are floats. Python tuple equality between int and float works if values equal, but string vs float no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if points[i] == points[j]: continue` but then if all points identical, D empty. We return N. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `if not D: return N` when N=0? We return before. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the original code's `D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))` with set of tuples was intended to handle duplicates by using set of points, but duplicates collapse. We changed to indices. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal lines if y1 is an int and for vertical lines if x1 is an int, but for nonvertical/nonhorizontal k1/k2 are floats. Python tuple equality between int and float works if values equal, but string vs float no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if points[i] == points[j]: continue` but then if all points identical, D empty. We return N. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `if not D: return N` when N=0? We return before. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's think about if the final code should be accepted by LeetCode. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is `-0.0`; hash equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `from collections import defaultdict` and `from typing import List`; if the judge already imports, no issue.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the original code's `D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))` with set of tuples was intended to handle duplicates by using set of points, but duplicates collapse. We changed to indices. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if points[i] == points[j]: continue` but then if all points identical, D empty. We return N. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `if not D: return N` when N=0? We return before. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's think about if the final code should be accepted by LeetCode. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is `-0.0`; hash equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `from collections import defaultdict` and `from typing import List`; if the judge already imports, no issue.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the original code's `D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))` with set of tuples was intended to handle duplicates by using set of points, but duplicates collapse. We changed to indices. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the algorithm's time complexity O(N^2) and memory O(N^2) for N=300, fine.

Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the algorithm's time complexity O(N^2) and memory O(N^2) for N=300, fine.

Now, let's think about if the final code should include `from typing import List`; yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the original code's `D = defaultdict(set)` and `return max([len(i) for i in D.values()])` would fail if D empty due to all identical. We fixed.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if not D: return N` but if N=2 and points distinct, D nonempty but return N before. If N=2 and points identical, return N. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `N` in `if not D: return N` when N is 0? Return before. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is `-0.0`; hash equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `points[i][0] == points[j][0] and points[i][1] == points[j][1]` to skip duplicates. We use x1,y1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the code if `points` is a list of lists and `points[i] == points[j]` is true for duplicate coordinates. We skip. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further optimized by not storing all lines but updating max. Not necessary.

Now, let's consider if there is a potential bug with the line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical if `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a float that is not exactly the same for different pairs due to floating point rounding. We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal lines if y1 is an int and for vertical lines if x1 is an int, but for nonvertical/nonhorizontal k1/k2 are floats. Python tuple equality between int and float works if values equal, but string vs float no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if points[i] == points[j]: continue` but then if all points identical, D empty. We return N. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `if not D: return N` when N=0? We return before. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `points[i][0] == points[j][0] and points[i][1] == points[j][1]` to skip duplicates. We use x1,y1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the code if `points` is a list of lists and `points[i] == points[j]` is true for duplicate coordinates. We skip. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further optimized by not storing all lines but updating max. Not necessary.

Now, let's consider if there is a potential bug with the line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical if `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a float that is not exactly the same for different pairs due to floating point rounding. We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal lines if y1 is an int and for vertical lines if x1 is an int, but for nonvertical/nonhorizontal k1/k2 are floats. Python tuple equality between int and float works if values equal, but string vs float no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if points[i] == points[j]: continue` but then if all points identical, D empty. We return N. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `if not D: return N` when N=0? We return before. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's think about if the final code should be accepted by LeetCode. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is `-0.0`; hash equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `from collections import defaultdict` and `from typing import List`; if the judge already imports, no issue.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the original code's `D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))` with set of tuples was intended to handle duplicates by using set of points, but duplicates collapse. We changed to indices. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal lines if y1 is an int and for vertical lines if x1 is an int, but for nonvertical/nonhorizontal k1/k2 are floats. Python tuple equality between int and float works if values equal, but string vs float no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if points[i] == points[j]: continue` but then if all points identical, D empty. We return N. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with `if not D: return N` when N=0? We return before. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's think about if the final code should be accepted by LeetCode. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is `-0.0`; hash equal. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `from collections import defaultdict` and `from typing import List`; if the judge already imports, no issue.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the original code's `D[(k1,k2)].add(tuple(points[i]))` with set of tuples was intended to handle duplicates by using set of points, but duplicates collapse. We changed to indices. Good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `max(len(v) for v in D.values())` and if D values are sets, len works. Good.

Now, let's think about if the final code should be accepted by LeetCode. Yes.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm with skip duplicate pairs and set of indices could be further simplified by not skipping duplicate pairs but using a different line key for duplicate pairs? No.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the algorithm's time complexity O(N^2) and memory O(N^2) for N=300, fine.

We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the algorithm's time complexity O(N^2) and memory O(N^2) for N=300, fine.

We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is `-0.0`; hash equal. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `points[i] == points[j]` to skip duplicates if points are lists and contain ints; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where all points are identical but N=1? Return 1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should handle the case where N=2 and points are identical? Return 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,0],[0,1]]. Algorithm:
i=0,j=1 skip.
i=0,j=2: x1==x2 (0==0), y1!=y2 -> key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
i=1,j=2: key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D[(0,'inf')]={0,1,2} len 3. return 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]] vertical line. Pairs:
(0,0)-(0,1): key (0,'inf') add 0,1.
(0,0)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 0,2.
(0,1)-(0,2): key (0,'inf') add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle the case where N=3 and points are [[0,0],[1,0],[2,0]] horizontal. Pairs:
(0,0)-(1,0): y1==y2 -> ('inf',0) add 0,1.
(0,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 0,2.
(1,0)-(2,0): ('inf',0) add 1,2.
D size 3. Correct.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal line if y1 is negative; key ('inf', -1). Fine.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` but if y1 is a float? no.

Now, let's consider if the final code should be accepted by a linter? Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden bug in the original code's line key formula for non-horizontal/non-vertical when `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a value that is not the intercept in the standard x = m y + b but negative. We verified.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code should use `(x2 * y1 - x1 * y2) / (y2 - y1)` or `(x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)`? The original uses the former. We verified it works with the negation. If we wanted to make it more standard, we could change to:
```
k1 = (x2 - x1) / (y2 - y1)
k2 = (x1 * y2 - x2 * y1) / (y2 - y1)
```
Then line equation x = k1*y + k2. This is more intuitive. But original uses negative. Should we fix it to standard? The original is not buggy, but maybe someone reading might think it's wrong. However, if we change to standard, we must ensure horizontal/vertical special cases align. For vertical line x=c, standard x = k1*y + k2 with k1=0,k2=c. But original special-cases vertical as (c,'inf'). If we change nonvertical formula to standard, vertical special-case key (c,'inf') would not collide with nonvertical lines with k1=0? Actually a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line cannot have k1=0 because that would be vertical. So no collision. But horizontal line y=c cannot be represented as x = k1*y + k2 because k1 infinite. Original special-cases horizontal as ('inf',c). So we could change nonvertical formula to standard and keep special cases. But then vertical line could also be represented by (0,c) if not special-cased, but special-cased as (c,'inf'). No collision. The original formula with negative is fine. Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the algorithm's time complexity O(N^2) and memory O(N^2) for N=300, fine.

Now, let's think about if the final code should include `from typing import List`; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `points[i][0] == points[j][0] and points[i][1] == points[j][1]` to skip duplicates. We use x1,y1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a potential bug with the line key for non-horizontal/non-vertical if `y2-y1` is negative and `k2` formula yields a float that is not exactly the same for different pairs due to floating point rounding. We argued safe. But let's test a near-collinear case with coordinates up to 10000:
Points (0,0), (9999,10000), (10000,9999) not collinear. Slopes for (0,0)-(9999,10000): k1=(0-9999)/(10000-0)=-0.9999, k2=0. For (0,0)-(10000,9999): k1=(0-10000)/(9999-0)=-1.00010001..., k2=0. Distinct. Good.
What about collinear points with large intercept: (0,10000), (1,9999), (2,9998) line x + y = 10000. For (0,10000)-(1,9999): y2-y1=-1, k1=(0-1)/(-1)=1, k2=(1*10000 - 0*9999)/(-1) = -10000. Key (1,-10000). For (0,10000)-(2,9998): y2-y1=-2, k1=(0-2)/(-2)=1, k2=(2*10000 - 0*9998)/(-2) = -10000. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `float` division but if coordinates are ints, `(x1 - x2) / (y2 - y1)` returns float. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the line key for horizontal lines if y1 is an int and for vertical lines if x1 is an int, but for nonvertical/nonhorizontal k1/k2 are floats. Python tuple equality between int and float works if values equal, but string vs float no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `if y1 == y2:` before `elif x1 == x2:`; if a line is vertical and y1==y2? That would mean duplicate point, skipped. So no.

Changing to standard might be a "broad rewrite" not needed. Keep original formula.

But wait, if we keep original formula and special-case vertical as (c,'inf'), then a vertical line key is (c,'inf'). A nonvertical/nonhorizontal line key is (k1,k2) where k1 = -m, k2 = -b. Could a nonvertical/nonhorizontal line have k1 = c (int) and k2 = 'inf'? no. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should use `k1, k2 = 'inf', y1` for horizontal. For a horizontal line, the nonstandard formula would have denominator zero, so special. For a vertical line, the nonstandard formula would actually work, but special-cased. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with the line key for horizontal lines if y1==y2 and x1!=x2, but if x1==x2 too (duplicate), skipped. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code should handle duplicate points by using `if x1 == x2 and y1 == y2: continue` before `if y1 == y2:`. Yes.
